Title: SILAGE: Memory-Efficient, Full-Gradient-Free Nonconvex Optimization for Nested Finite Sums

URL Source: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.15832v2

Published Time: Fri, 19 Jun 2026 00:42:37 GMT

Number of Pages: 81

Markdown Content:
# SILAGE: Memory-Efficient, Full-Gradient-Free Nonconvex Optimization for Nested Finite Sums 

Igor Sokolov 

KAUST *

Laurent Condat 

KAUST 

Peter Richtárik 

KAUST 

## Abstract 

Empirical risk minimization on massive datasets naturally exhibits a nested double finite-sum structure, where 𝑁 = 𝑛𝑚 total samples are logically or physically partitioned into 𝑛 blocks of size 𝑚 (e.g., in pooled data silos, out-of-core learn-ing, or deliberate stratification). While variance-reduced methods achieve optimal oracle complexities for nonconvex objectives, they suffer from severe scaling bot-tlenecks in this centralized regime. Recursive estimators, such as PAGE , require periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all 𝑛𝑚 samples, which are computa-tionally expensive. Conversely, single-loop methods, such as SILVER , avoid such refreshes but require an impractical 𝒪 (𝑛𝑚 ) memory footprint to store a control variate for every sample. In this paper, we propose SILAGE , a variance-reduced algorithm that addresses this trade-off. By actively exploiting the double-sum structure, SILAGE eliminates periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all 𝑛𝑚 

components—evaluating at most one local group gradient per iteration—while requiring only 𝒪 (𝑛 ) memory. Furthermore, we provide a tight convergence anal-ysis that avoids pessimistic worst-case Lipschitz constants. Instead, SILAGE ’s complexity natively adapts to the underlying data geometry via nested functional similarities: across-group ( 𝛿 1) and within-group ( 𝛿 2) heterogeneity. Our results improve existing state-of-the-art bounds in several practically relevant regimes. 

## 1 Introduction 

1.1 The centralized double-sum paradigm 

Nonconvex optimization is ubiquitous across all areas of machine learning and artificial intelligence. In particular, empirical risk minimization (ERM) is the dominant paradigm in modern large-scale settings for supervised model training. While ERM is typically formulated as a flat finite sum of 𝑁 

components, modern centralized training pipelines increasingly exhibit a nested double-sum structure 

𝑁 = 𝑛𝑚 , where data are logically or physically partitioned into 𝑛 groups, blocks, or silos, each containing 𝑚 samples [19, 28, 11, 5, 35, 47, 40]. Thus, we consider nonconvex double finite-sum problems of the form 

min

The possibly nonconvex function 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 : R𝑑 → R typically represents the loss on the 𝑗 -th sample in the 𝑖 -th group, and 𝑓 𝑖 is the averaged objective function of that specific group. Our analysis assumes that the aggregate objective 𝑓 is lower bounded and 𝐿 -smooth for some 𝐿 > 0.Crucially, however, we do not require every individual 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 to be smooth, in contrast to many existing

methods (see Section 2 for our detailed assumptions). While standard gradient descent solves (1) with a gradient evaluation complexity 2 of 𝒪 (︀ 𝑛𝑚𝐿 Δ0

> 𝜖

)︀ , variance-reduced (VR) stochastic gradient methods are now standard tools for reducing this complexity [16, 12, 25, 36, 41, 39]. Exploiting this two-layer partition is vital because it naturally maps to several critical, single-machine settings where standard flat VR methods fail to scale [38, 3, 14]: • Data Silos and Pooled Data Lakes: Imagine a centralized data center where data are legally or physically partitioned into 𝑛 distinct “silos.” For example, a healthcare consortium aggregating patient records from 𝑛 different hospitals, or an enterprise training on 𝑛 different data modalities (text, images, logs) stored on a centralized cluster. While the computation is centralized—avoiding the communication latency and synchronization bottlenecks of federated learning—the data inher-ently retain their siloed structure. Each silo 𝑖 contains 𝑚 samples that share regional demographics or domain-specific features. • Out-of-Core and Memory-Constrained Learning: In massive-scale training, such as optimizing foundation models on billions of tokens [ 32 , 4 ], the dataset 𝑁 vastly exceeds GPU VRAM or main memory. Data must be stored on slower NVMe/SSDs and loaded in chunks. Here, 𝑛 represents the number of physical storage blocks (or shards), and 𝑚 is the block size. Algorithms that require maintaining a state (control variate) for every single sample 𝑗 ∈ [𝑁 ] incur an 𝒪 (𝑁 ) memory footprint, which is impractical in this regime. Conversely, storing just 𝑛 control variates (one per block) reduces this memory overhead by a factor of 𝑚 .• Deliberate Stratification and Clustering: Even if the data are provided as a flat array, an optimizer can actively induce a double-sum structure to accelerate training. By clustering data via lightweight preprocessing—such as grouping by class labels or applying 𝑘 -means to input features—the dataset is partitioned into 𝑛 highly homogeneous clusters. This stratification creates a favorable variance geometry that our algorithm can natively exploit. We emphasize that our setting is grouped (nested) finite-sum optimization in the centralized regime: federated and client-partitioned data serve as motivating special cases rather than the object of study, and our guarantees bound gradient (oracle) complexity and memory, not communication complexity. 

1.2 Related work and contributions 

All existing VR estimators exhibit scaling limitations at massive sizes, forcing a difficult trade-off between computation and memory. The first category consists of methods that necessitate periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all 

𝑛𝑚 samples. This includes estimators such as SVRG [16 , 20 ], SCSG [ 22 ], and SSRGD [ 24 ], as well as recursive estimators such as SARAH [30 ], SPIDER [ 12 ], SpiderBoost [ 46 ], and PAGE [ 25 , 44 ] (also independently proposed as Loopless-SARAH ( L2S ) [ 23 ]). Periodic global full-gradient refreshes are expensive and limit the scalability of these methods on very large datasets. The second category comprises memory-based alternatives, such as classical SAG /SAGA -type es-timators [ 37 , 9, 2, 10 ] and recent methods like ZeroSARAH [26 ] and SILVER [ 31 ]. These methods successfully avoid periodic global full-gradient refreshes. However, when applied to a flattened dataset, they suffer from an impractical 𝒪 (𝑛𝑚𝑑 ) memory footprint required to store a 𝑑 -dimensional control variate for every individual sample. Thus, in the centralized large-scale regime considered here, the primary bottlenecks are gradient evaluation complexity and memory footprint . The 𝑛 -group structure lets us navigate this trade-off. We introduce SILAGE (SIngle Loop Average Gradient Estimator ), a novel variance-reduced algorithm tailored for double finite-sum problems. Our contributions are organized along three structural axes— memory footprint, avoidance of periodic global full-gradient refreshes, and dependence of the convergence rate on the data geometry—together with a unification result that places SILAGE within the existing landscape: • Memory Efficiency via Nested Structure: By maintaining 𝑛 control-variate table en-tries (one per block), plus 𝒪 (1) auxiliary 𝑑 -vectors—namely the running aggregate

𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 and 𝑛 > 𝑚 ) that explicitly leverage nested similarity constants: 𝛿 1 (across-group) and 𝛿 2 (within-group). This structured decomposition is fundamentally more expressive and informative than standard flattened similarity bounds (detailed in Section 2). • Exact Limiting Reductions (unification): Under matching parameter choices, SILAGE 

unifies existing approaches by exactly recovering PAGE (when 𝑛 = 1 ) and SILVER (when 

𝑚 = 1 , 𝑏 = 1 ), hence the algorithm’s name. At the level of building blocks, SILAGE inherits two standard ideas: PAGE -style recursive gradient tracking and the stored control variates of memory-based ( SAGA /SILVER -style) estimators. Its novelty lies in how these are combined and analyzed for the nested problem: (i) an estimator coupling that drives all 𝑛 group estimators through a single global coin and one uniformly sampled group, so that at most one group is fully refreshed per iteration; (ii) storing one control variate per group rather than per sample, giving the 𝒪 (𝑛 ) footprint in place of the flattened 𝒪 (𝑛𝑚 ); (iii) a shared-drift update that handles the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime by correcting un-sampled groups without fresh per-group gradients; and (iv) a two-level nonconvex analysis in the nested similarity constants 𝛿 1,𝛿 2.Table 1 contrasts SILAGE with representative baselines for nonconvex (double) finite-sum optimization along these axes, summarizing the contributions above. ## 2 Assumptions 

We first recall standard assumptions from nonconvex optimization. The following two assumptions are classical and are used in essentially the same form in the analysis of gradient descent and modern variance-reduced methods for nonconvex finite sums [29, 25, 26, 44]. 

Assumption 1 (Lower boundedness) . There exists 𝑓 inf ∈ R such that 𝑓 (𝑥 ) ≥ 𝑓 inf for all 𝑥 ∈ R𝑑 .

Assumption 2 (Smoothness of the average objective) . There exists 𝐿 ≥ 0 such that, for all 𝑥,𝑦 ∈ R𝑑 ,

‖∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) − ∇ 𝑓 (𝑦 )‖ ≤ 𝐿 ‖𝑥 − 𝑦 ‖ .

In float64 precision this is approximately 8𝑛𝑑 bytes for large 𝑛 , ignoring lower-order implementation overhead such as sampled indices, array metadata, and temporary workspace.

> (1)

Full-gradient-free : the method’s theoretical guarantees do not require periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all 𝑛𝑚 components to reach an 𝜖 -stationary point. A full-gradient-free method may still evaluate at most one local group gradient ∇𝑓 𝑖 per iteration; SILAGE does so at each anchor reset, at 𝒪 (𝑚 ) cost. The one-time 𝑛𝑚 cost of exact initialization, used only for the like-for-like comparison in Section 3.3.1 (Table 3), is separate and is not required by SILAGE (Corollaries 1 and 2).  

> (2)

Nested structure : the method explicitly exploits the nested double finite-sum structure of the objective.  

> (3)

Memory footprint : the worst-case number of additional 𝑑 -dimensional vectors (control variates) maintained throughout execution, reported up to universal constants.  

> (4)

Similarity : the type of functional-similarity assumption underlying the convergence analysis (flattened, nested, or none).  

> (5)

Note : a brief additional remark on the method.

For SILVER , PAGE , and SILAGE ,the table reports the algorithm exactly recovered when 𝑚 = 1 or 𝑛 = 1 .A key point of our setting is that Assumption 2 is imposed strictly on the average objective 𝑓 . Unless explicitly stated otherwise, we do not require every component 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 to be individually smooth; they are merely assumed to be differentiable. Instead, we control the variance geometry of the double finite sum in (1) through two nested similarity conditions. 

Assumption 3 (Across-group similarity) . There exists 𝛿 1 ≥ 0 such that, for all 𝑥,𝑦 ∈ R𝑑 ,

(3) Assumption 3 measures how much the group gradients deviate from the global gradient, while Assumption 4 measures the average deviation of sample gradients from their respective group averages. 4These are structural conditions of a different nature than component-wise smoothness: the latter is one sufficient condition for them, yet they can be much smaller than the worst-case component smoothness in favorable grouped and statistical regimes. For example, if all 𝑓 𝑖 are 𝐿 1-smooth, then (2) holds with 𝛿 1 ≤ 𝐿 1; similarly, a mean-squared smoothness bound on the individual functions 

𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 implies (3) . In many distributed and statistical regimes, similarity constants can be substantially smaller than pessimistic smoothness bounds [ 7, 21 , 1, 42 , 15 , 18 , 17 , 6]. Furthermore, in i.i.d. ERM settings, related similarity quantities decrease proportionately with the local sample size under standard statistical assumptions [ 45 , 48 ]. Importantly, SILAGE still assumes the average objective 

𝑓 is 𝐿 -smooth (Assumption 2), and only avoids dependence on component-wise or worst-case smoothness. Conversely, a flat bound controls both nested residuals, but the minimal constants on the two sides need not be related by a clean equality, since a supremum over 𝑥,𝑦 of a sum need not equal the sum of the individual suprema. The two-parameter representation by the pair (𝛿 1,𝛿 2) is therefore strictly more informative and provides a significantly finer algorithmic characterization. For instance, if there is no within-group variation, then 𝛿 2 = 0 and 𝛿 flat = 𝛿 1; conversely, if there is no across-group variation, then 𝛿 1 = 0 and 𝛿 flat = 𝛿 2. As we will demonstrate, SILAGE successfully decouples these dependencies depending on the operational regime. In the optimization literature, component-level smoothness assumptions are typically formulated as follows [25, 44, 26, 43, 49]: 

‖∇ 𝑓 𝑖 (𝑥 ) − ∇ 𝑓 𝑖 (𝑦 )‖ ≤ 𝐿 𝑖 ‖𝑥 − 𝑦 ‖ , ∀𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ R𝑑 , (5) 

‖∇ 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) − ∇ 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑦 )‖ ≤ 𝐿 𝑖,𝑗 ‖𝑥 − 𝑦 ‖ , ∀𝑥, 𝑦 ∈ R𝑑 , (6) for some constants 𝐿 𝑖 , 𝐿 𝑖,𝑗 < ∞. The worst-case component smoothness is commonly denoted by 

𝐿 max := max 𝑖,𝑗 𝐿 𝑖,𝑗 . Additionally, some analyses utilize an averaged smoothness assumption:

‖∇ 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) − ∇ 𝑓 𝑖 (𝑥 ) − ∇ 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑦 ) + ∇𝑓 𝑖 (𝑦 )‖2 ≤ 𝛿 22,𝑖 ‖𝑥 − 𝑦 ‖2 . (8) We emphasize that the total oracle complexity of SILAGE depends strictly on 𝐿 , 𝛿 1, and 𝛿 2, entirely bypassing any dependence on the potentially huge worst-case constant 𝐿 max . This structural advan-tage makes our theoretical rates significantly tighter in heterogeneous regimes compared to existing component-smoothness approaches. The fundamental novelty of considering two layers of similarity ( 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2) is not that their combina-tion is always strictly smaller than the flattened similarity. Rather, Assumptions 3 and 4 rigorously decompose the flattened residual into distinct between-group and within-group components. As we demonstrate in our complexity analysis, SILAGE natively exploits this explicit separation: depending on the dataset dimensions, either 𝛿 2 alone dictates the leading complexity (in the 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 regime), or both constants enter as multipliers of different dimensional terms (in the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime). To the best of our knowledge, prior variance-reduction guarantees for double finite sums do not yield this two-level dependence on both 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2 while maintaining an 𝒪 (𝑛 ) memory footprint via a single estimator per group. This dual-parameterization (𝛿 1,𝛿 2) provides a fundamentally more informative view of the geometry of (1) than a single flattened similarity constant. By decoupling distinct sources of heterogeneity— where 𝛿 1 measures the variation of the group objectives 𝑓 𝑖 around the global objective 𝑓 , and 𝛿 2

measures the average variation of the component functions 𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 around their local group averages 

𝑓 𝑖 —this framework naturally captures several practically relevant data regimes: • Globally and locally homogeneous data ( 𝛿 1 small, 𝛿 2 small). This regime arises when the groups are mutually similar and each group is internally coherent. Examples include repeated measurements collected under nearly identical experimental conditions, data acquired across standardized sites following the same protocol, or stable temporal windows of a slowly varying process. In such settings, both across-group and within-group deviations are mild. • Redundant blocks ( 𝛿 1 small, 𝛿 2 relatively large). This regime is induced by the random sharding of a large dataset: each group behaves as a representative subsample of the global population. Consequently, different groups are statistically close to one another ( 𝛿 1 is small), while the internal sample-level diversity inside each group remains substantial ( 𝛿 2 is large relative to 𝛿 1). This situation is common in distributed ERM when data are partitioned purely for computational scaling rather than semantic grouping. • Homogeneous silos ( 𝛿 1 large, 𝛿 2 small). Here, each group is internally coherent, but different groups correspond to genuinely distinct populations, domains, tasks, or modalities. Examples include clustered federated learning, client-specific personalization, or task-based partitions. Within-group variability is minimal, but the discrepancy between local objectives is pronounced. • Heterogeneous silos ( 𝛿 1 large, 𝛿 2 large). This regime captures challenging large-scale settings in which different groups correspond to distinct populations and each individ-ual group is itself internally diverse. Examples include hierarchical federated learning architectures [ 27 ] spanning institutions with different demographics and broad case mixes, cross-market recommendation systems, or multi-domain corpora. In these cases, both levels of heterogeneity are substantial. 6Algorithm 1 SILAGE (new ), case 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛

A single flattened similarity constant inherently obscures these qualitatively different geometries. By contrast, the (𝛿 1,𝛿 2) pair makes the multiscale heterogeneity explicit, allowing the resulting oracle complexity bounds to cleanly separate the structural impact of across-group and within-group variation. Crucially, SILAGE is agnostic to these parameters during execution; it does not require prior knowl-edge of whether 𝛿 1 or 𝛿 2 dominates. It naturally adapts to whatever underlying data geometry is present, yielding theoretical complexities that strictly improve a posteriori based on the relative tightness of 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2.

## 3 SILAGE Algorithm and Convergence Results 

To obtain a single-loop estimator that entirely avoids periodic global full-gradient refreshes while exploiting the two-level structure of (1) , SILAGE relies on a carefully constructed fusion of two variance-reduction philosophies: it applies a SILVER -like memory structure across the 𝑛 groups, and a PAGE -like recursive tracking within the 𝑚 components of each group. However, naively deploying 

𝑛 independent PAGE estimators (one for each group) would fail to yield a practical algorithm. In standard PAGE , an estimator is refreshed with the exact gradient with probability 1/𝑚 . If we ran 𝑛 

independent coin flips per iteration, the number of groups triggering a full refresh simultaneously would be a binomial random variable. Consequently, the number of component gradients evaluated per iteration would be 𝑛𝑚 in the worst case, which is precisely what we want to avoid. To guarantee a strictly bounded maximum batch size, SILAGE carefully couples the two strategies. The group estimators are correlated in a specific way that depends on the aspect ratio of the dataset: • The 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 regime (Algorithm 1): Our design target is that each of the 𝑛 group estimators be fully refreshed with average probability 1/𝑚 per iteration, matching the refresh rate of a PAGE estimator on a block of 𝑚 components. To meet this target while never refreshing more than one group per iteration, we couple a single global coin with a uniform group selection: with probability 𝑝 the coin lands heads, and conditioned on heads we select exactly one group 𝑖 𝑡 uniformly at random to undergo a full local refresh ∇𝑓 𝑖 𝑡 (𝑥 𝑡 +1 ). Under this scheme, the marginal probability that any fixed group is refreshed in a given iteration is 𝑝 · 1 

> 𝑛

; equating this with the target rate 1/𝑚 yields 

𝑝 = 𝑛/𝑚 , which is a valid probability precisely because 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 . Thus Algorithm 1 evaluates 7at most one local group gradient ∇𝑓 𝑖 𝑡 per iteration, and only on reset iterations (those for which the coin lands heads); consequently, the maximum batch size stays at 𝒪 (𝑚 + 𝑛 ) = 𝒪 (𝑚 ). In this regime, the PAGE estimator is fully operational on each block independently; thus, the blocks do not interfere with each other, and across-group heterogeneity ( 𝛿 1) does not affect the leading complexity. • The 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime (Algorithm 2): The previous strategy fails because 𝑛/𝑚 > 1. To maintain an 𝒪 (𝑚 ) per-iteration cost, we cannot compute multiple (ideally 𝑛/𝑚 ) group refreshes ∇𝑓 𝑖 , so we compute exactly one. Moreover, updating the remaining 𝑛 − 1 estimators with fresh local data would cost 𝒪 (𝑛 ), breaking our 𝒪 (𝑚 ) budget. We circumvent this by selecting only a small subset of 𝑏 groups (typically 𝑏 = 𝑚 ) to receive fresh gradient evaluations. There is no gradient evaluation for the remaining un-sampled groups; instead, their estimators are updated using a shared drift 

computed exclusively from the 𝑏 active groups. We pay a theoretical price for this cost-saving design: because the un-sampled groups rely on updates generated by other groups, the across-group heterogeneity ( 𝛿 1) enters the convergence complexity. At the exact boundary where 𝑛 = 𝑚 , both formulations naturally coincide under the right parameter choices. In the following subsections, we state the convergence guarantees for both regimes. Both versions store exactly one estimator 𝑔 𝑡 𝑖 for each group 𝑓 𝑖 and use their average 𝑔 𝑡 := 1

> 𝑛

∑︀ 𝑛 𝑖 =1 𝑔 𝑡 𝑖 as the descent direction.

Because this shared correction forces estimators to track directions generated by different groups, its variance relies on both the across-group ( 𝛿 1) and within-group ( 𝛿 2) heterogeneities.

The drift update in Algorithm 2 is written to touch all 𝑛 estimators, which is convenient for the analysis but would suggest an 𝒪 (𝑛 ) per-iteration cost. Appendix D.1 removes this overhead: storing each estimator in the shifted form 𝑔 𝑡 𝑖 = ℎ𝑡 𝑖 + 𝑞 𝑡 with a single shared accumulator 𝑞 𝑡 , the equivalent Algorithm 3 applies the shared drift via one update of 𝑞 𝑡 and refreshes only the ˜Ω𝑡 active entries; Lemma 9 proves the two forms generate identical iterates, so the implementation realizes the 

𝒪 (𝑚 + 𝑏 grp ) per-iteration cost. Note that when 𝑏 grp = 𝑚 , the coefficient multiplying 𝛿 1 becomes 𝑚 √𝑛 − 𝑚 . This term vanishes at the exact boundary 𝑛 = 𝑚 , yielding algebraic equivalence with the bounds from the 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 regime. At the opposite extreme, when 𝑚 = 1 and 𝑏 grp = 1 , the subset Ω𝑡 is empty, and SILAGE reverts to 

SILVER , with our complexity matching the one in Oko et al. [31].

93.3 Comparison with closely related baselines 

In this section, we compare the theoretical guarantees of SILAGE against closely related variance-reduced baselines under exact initialization. This is the fairest main-text comparison to methods that begin from the true full gradient, while keeping the core discussion focused. The complementary comparison under arbitrary initialization is deferred to Appendix A.2. 

3.3.1 Exact initialization (full-gradient) 

We now compare the algorithms under exact initialization, where 𝑔 0 

> 𝑖

= ∇𝑓 𝑖 (𝑥 0) for all 𝑖 ∈ [𝑛 ]. In this setting, 𝑔 0 = ∇𝑓 (𝑥 0) and the initial Lyapunov quantity perfectly collapses to Ψ0 = ∆ 0 := 

𝑓 (𝑥 0) − 𝑓 inf , at the cost of 𝑛𝑚 component-gradient evaluations. We emphasize that SILAGE does not require exact initialization to enjoy its theoretical convergence guarantees: arbitrary initial estimators 𝑔 0 

> 𝑖

are admissible, and the initialization error enters the complexity only through the Lyapunov quantity Ψ0. The exact initialization adopted here is used solely for a like-for-like comparison with baselines that themselves rely on it. To avoid overloading batch-size notation in the comparison, we write 𝑏 fp for the flattened PAGE 

minibatch size, 𝑏 sv for the SILVER minibatch size, 𝑏 zs for the ZeroSARAH minibatch size, (𝑠 dp ,𝑏 dp )

for the outer/client and local batch sizes of D-PAGE , (𝑠 dzs ,𝑏 dzs ) for the client and local batch sizes of 

> D-ZeroSARAH

, and 𝑏 grp for the group-batch parameter of SILAGE in the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime; this is the parameter that appears in Algorithm 2, Theorem 2, and Corollary 2. and 𝑚𝐿 + √𝑛𝑚 𝛿 flat , respectively. Like flattened SILVER , this inherently merges nested variability into a single constant. 11 The structured double-Nice D-PAGE baseline is considerably stronger. With outer/client batch size

• Regime 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 : The useful full-outer-sampling choice 𝑠 dp = 𝑛 and 𝑏 dp = 1 gives a leading factor of 𝑛𝐿 + √𝑛𝑚 ¯𝛿 2. This matches the leading SILAGE dependence up to the distinction between 𝛿 2 and ¯𝛿 2. However, D-PAGE pays for this with periodic global full-gradient refreshes. Furthermore, since 𝛿 2 ≤ ¯𝛿 2 (above), with ¯𝛿 2 averaging client-wise worst-case constants and 𝛿 2 a global within-group average, SILAGE is weakly preferable in this regime, and the gap can be substantial in heterogeneous settings. • Regime 𝑛 > 𝑚 : The choice 𝑠 dp = 𝑚 and 𝑏 dp = 1 gives a leading factor of 𝑚𝐿 +√︀ 

𝑚 (𝑛 − 𝑚 ) 𝛿 1 + √𝑛𝑚 ¯𝛿 2, against 𝑚𝐿 + 𝑚 √𝑛 − 𝑚 𝛿 1 + √𝑛𝑚 𝛿 2 for the 𝑏 grp = 𝑚 

specialization of SILAGE . D-PAGE is a strong full-refresh baseline here, and the two are not 

ordered coefficient-wise: D-PAGE has the smaller across-group coefficient ( √︀ 𝑚 (𝑛 − 𝑚 )

versus SILAGE ’s 𝑚 √𝑛 − 𝑚 , a factor of √𝑚 ), whereas SILAGE has the smaller within-group constant, since 𝛿 2 ≤ ¯𝛿 2. Thus SILAGE improves on D-PAGE only when its within-group advantage outweighs this across-group penalty, i.e. whenever 𝑚 √𝑛 − 𝑚 𝛿 1 + √𝑛𝑚 𝛿 2 ≪√︀ 

𝑚 (𝑛 − 𝑚 ) 𝛿 1 + √𝑛𝑚 ¯𝛿 2, and it does so without the periodic global full-gradient refreshes  

> D-PAGE

requires. Ultimately, the cleanest distinction between SILAGE and PAGE -type methods is algorithmic rather than a uniform oracle-complexity improvement. D-PAGE can attain competitive leading factors and keeps a flat 𝒪 (1) memory footprint, but, like every PAGE -type estimator, it requires periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all 𝑛𝑚 components. SILAGE is instead single-loop: it removes these refreshes entirely, at the cost of 𝒪 (𝑛 ) group-level memory (one estimator per group), while still exploiting the nested similarity structure. Its advantage over D-PAGE is therefore the elimination of periodic global refreshes—most valuable when such refreshes dominate cost—rather than a claim of improving every oracle-complexity term. The constants 𝐿 , 𝛿 1, and 𝛿 2 are objects of the analysis : they determine the largest provably admissible stepsize and the resulting convergence rate, but they do not appear in the algorithm itself. SILAGE 

samples groups, flips the global coin, and performs anchor resets and recursive updates without ever referencing them, so a single run is identical regardless of their values. Calibrating a stepsize to problem constants is standard for variance-reduced methods— 𝐿 for gradient descent, 𝐿 max for 

> ZeroSARAH

—and the distinguishing feature of SILAGE is not independence from such constants but dependence on the structured quantities 𝐿,𝛿 1,𝛿 2 rather than the pessimistic 𝐿 max or the flattened 

𝛿 flat ; this is precisely what yields the larger admissible stepsize and the improved rates established above. In practice, the constants may be estimated whenever curvature access is available—as for the logistic-regression objectives in our experiments, where Hessian–vector products make 𝐿,𝛿 1,𝛿 2 cheap to probe (Appendix E)—replaced by any valid upper bound, or sidestepped by a standard stepsize search, all without altering the algorithm. Our experiments adopt the first route: probe-set estimates are used in the theoretically admissible stepsize while batch sizes are tuned per method, so that no exact prior knowledge of these constants is assumed.

Figure 2: Optimization trajectories on the controlled nonconvex-regularized logistic regression task, 

𝑛 > 𝑚 regime ( 𝑛 = 250 , 𝑚 = 50 ). Each panel fixes one of the four similarity regimes; the corresponding measured constants (𝛿 1,𝛿 2,𝐿,𝐿 max ) are reported in Table 4. We compare SILAGE against three baselines that successfully avoid periodic global full-gradient refreshes: flattened ZeroSARAH [ 26 ], flattened SILVER [ 31 ], and the nested distributed variant  

> D-ZeroSARAH

[26 ]. We intentionally omit PAGE and D-PAGE , whose estimators fundamentally rely on periodic global full-gradient refreshes over all 𝑛𝑚 components [ 25 , 44 ]; the empirical comparison thus isolates the no-refresh design class, while the oracle-cost of those refreshes is quantified analytically in Section 3.3.1 (Table 3). We deploy Algorithm 1 for the 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 regime and Algorithm 2 for the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime. All methods use the largest theoretically admissible constant stepsize from their respective analyses, evaluated with empirical estimates of the relevant smoothness and similarity constants; batch-size parameters are tuned per method, with the full protocol (probe-set estimation, tuning grids, and fallback rule) described in Appendix E.6. For SILAGE in the 𝑛 > 𝑚 

regime, the headline specialization 𝑏 grp = 𝑚 gives a clean closed-form rate but is not necessarily oracle-optimal: Theorem 2 holds for every 𝑏 grp ∈ [𝑛 ], the oracle-optimal choice is problem-dependent (Appendix D.4), and we accordingly tune 𝑏 grp over a grid, so the selected value can be smaller than 

𝑚 . We report the exact squared gradient norm, ‖∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 𝑡 )‖2, recomputed every two iterations, until reaching ‖∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 𝑡 )‖2 ≤ 10 −12 or exhausting the epoch budget. Across these controlled synthetic runs, Figures 1 and 2 show SILAGE to be the fastest, or among the fastest, of the tested methods, with the empirical trajectories closely tracking our theoretical predictions. In the 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 regime (Figure 1), our theory indicates the leading similarity term depends entirely on 𝛿 2, ignoring 𝛿 1. The plots are consistent with this: in both small-𝛿 2 panels, SILAGE 

converges rapidly even when the across-group heterogeneity 𝛿 1 is large. While a large 𝛿 2 naturally increases the problem difficulty, SILAGE still outperforms both ZeroSARAH variants and matches or exceeds SILVER in these runs. In the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime (Figure 2), where both 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2 factor into the bound, SILAGE outperforms the tested baselines across all four similarity profiles shown. Taken together, these controlled experiments support our theoretical predictions, indicating a practical benefit from explicitly separating and exploiting nested similarity rather than relying on flattened structures or pessimistic worst-case component smoothness. 14 We emphasize that these experiments are a controlled synthetic validation of the predicted similarity-dependence rather than a systems study: the 𝒪 (𝑛 ) memory and scalability advantages are established by construction and summarized in Table 1, not measured here, and a real grouped-data or wall-clock evaluation is left to future work. 

## 5 Additional Results 

Several complementary results are deferred to the appendix. A full description of the experimental setup—covering data-generation protocols, the Hessian-vector-product estimation of 𝐿 , 𝛿 1, and 

𝛿 2, hyperparameter tuning, and implementation details—is provided in Appendix E. The Polyak– Łojasiewicz convergence analyses of SILAGE appear in Appendix C.2 for the 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 regime and in Appendix D.3 for the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime. Finally, the corresponding complexity comparison against the same baselines under arbitrary (zero-full-gradient) initialization is given in Appendix A.2.

## A Additional Comparison with Closely Related Baselines 

A.1 Exact initialization (full-gradient) 

For readability, the main-text Table 3 reports only the specialization of each method’s oracle com-plexity under canonical batch-size choices, and omits the choices themselves. For completeness, Table 5 below reproduces the same comparison but now also includes the general tunable form of each bound, as a function of the method-specific batch-size parameters introduced in Section 3.3.1:

for D-ZeroSARAH , and 𝑏 grp for SILAGE in the 𝑛 > 𝑚 regime. Comparison with SILVER . The oracle complexity of flattened SILVER is

> 𝑛𝑚

∑︀ 𝑛𝑚 𝑟 =1 ⃦⃦⃦ ∇ ˜𝑓 𝑟 (𝑥 0) − ∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 0)⃦⃦⃦ 2

measures the initial variance. Assuming a low-initial-variance regime where Ψ0 and ∆0 are comparable, SILAGE improves upon optimized, zero-initialized flattened SILVER under the following conditions: • Regime 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 : SILAGE is strictly better when 𝑛𝐿 + √𝑛𝑚 𝛿 2 ≪ √𝑛𝑚 𝛿 flat , or equivalently √︀ 

𝑛/𝑚𝐿 + 𝛿 2 ≪ 𝛿 flat . This occurs when flattening is inherently costly—for instance, when the flat heterogeneity 𝛿 flat is primarily driven by across-group variation ( 𝛿 1), which SILAGE entirely avoids paying for in this regime. If instead 𝛿 flat ≈ 𝛿 2 and 𝜎 2flat is small, the theoretical rate matches 

SILVER , but SILAGE retains its massive 𝒪 (𝑛 ) rather than 𝒪 (𝑛𝑚 ) memory advantage. • Regime 𝑛 > 𝑚 : Comparing the leading factors for a chosen group batch size 𝑏 grp ∈ [𝑚,𝑛 ],

SILAGE is favorable when (𝑚 + 𝑏 grp )(︂ 𝐿 +√︀ 𝑛 − 𝑏 grp 𝛿 1 +√︁ 𝑛 𝑏 grp 𝛿 2)︂ ≪ √𝑛𝑚 𝛿 flat . For our targeted choice 𝑏 grp = 𝑚 , this simplifies to √︀ 𝑚/𝑛𝐿 +√︀ 𝑚 (𝑛 − 𝑚 )/𝑛 𝛿 1 + 𝛿 2 ≪ 𝛿 flat . Thus, the small-batch choice is highly effective when the scaled across-group term is small. If 𝛿 1 is large, increasing 𝑏 grp to 𝑛 eliminates the across-group term entirely, proving preferable whenever 

𝑚 √𝑛 − 𝑚 𝛿 1 ≳ (𝑛 − 𝑚 )𝐿 + ( 𝑛 − √𝑛𝑚 )𝛿 2 (ignoring initial Ψ0 differences). 21 B Auxiliary results 

This section collects several auxiliary results that we repeatedly use in the proofs. Most of them are elementary identities for smooth functions, conditional second moments, and uniform sampling without replacement; we state them explicitly to keep the main proof steps focused on the estimator recursions. 

Lemma 1 (Inexact-gradient descent; [ 25 ]) . Let 𝑓 be 𝐿 -smooth, let 𝛾 > 0, and let 𝑥 + = 𝑥 − 𝛾𝑔 for some 𝑥,𝑔 ∈ R𝑑 . Then

2⃦⃦ 𝑥 + − 𝑥 ⃦⃦ 2 .

∇𝑓 (𝑥 ), 𝑥 + − 𝑥 ⟩︀ = 𝛾

gives the claim.

C.2 Analysis under the Polyak–Łojasiewicz condition 

We next record the PŁ specialization for the case 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 . The algorithm and the estimator recursion are unchanged, but the PŁ inequality turns the descent estimate into a contraction for the objective gap. Thus the accuracy dependence becomes 𝑂 (︀ · log 1

> 𝜖

)︀ for reaching E[︀ 𝑓 (𝑥 𝑇 ) − 𝑓 *]︀ ≤ 𝜖 , instead of the 𝑂 (︀ · 1

> 𝜖

)︀ dependence in the preceding general nonconvex stationarity bounds. The theorem is stated under a global PŁ condition. However, the proof uses this condition only at the iterates, so the same argument applies on any PŁ region that contains the trajectory. We state the PŁ condition once here because it is shared by the PŁ analyses in both regimes. 

Assumption 5 (Polyak–Łojasiewicz (PŁ) condition) . The function 𝑓 satisfies the PŁ-condition with parameter 𝜇 > 0 if 

‖∇ 𝑓 (𝑥 )‖2 ≥ 2𝜇 (︀ 𝑓 (𝑥 ) − 𝑓 *)︀ , ∀𝑥 ∈ R𝑑 , (18)

D.1 Implementation-efficient form and equivalence 

The algorithm below is an implementation-efficient form of Algorithm 2. It avoids explicitly updating all table entries by storing a shared shift, while keeping the same iterates as the analysis-friendly version. Algorithm 2 is the form used in the analysis, while Algorithm 3 is its implementation-efficient form. They are linked by the reparameterization

Coupling of the randomness. To compare the two procedures pathwise, we run them with the same random draws at every iteration: the same index 𝑖 𝑡 ∈ [𝑛 ], the same subset Ω𝑡 ⊂ [𝑛 ] ∖ { 𝑖 𝑡 } of size 𝑏 grp − 1, and the same component indices {𝑗 𝑡 𝑖 }𝑖 ∈ ˜Ω𝑡 , where ˜Ω𝑡 := Ω 𝑡 ∪ { 𝑖 𝑡 }. We justify that, despite being generated in two stages, the set ˜Ω𝑡 is uniformly distributed. Recall the sampling procedure in Algorithm 2: first, we draw 𝑖 𝑡 ∼ 𝒰 ([ 𝑛 ]) . D.3 Analysis under the Polyak–Łojasiewicz condition 

The PŁ argument for the regime 𝑛 > 𝑚 is parallel. The only change is in the constant controlling the Lyapunov recursion: both similarity parameters now enter through the batch-dependent quantity 

Ξ𝑛,𝑏 grp below. Under Assumption 5, this gives an 𝑂 (︀ · log 1

> 𝜖

)︀ dependence for the objective gap, rather than the 𝑂 (︀ · 1

> 𝜖

)︀ dependence of the general nonconvex stationarity result. As above, the theorem is stated globally, while the proof only invokes PŁ along the iterates; local PŁ regions are therefore sufficient whenever the trajectory stays inside them. The next theorem is the PŁ analogue of Theorem 2. The quantity Ξ𝑛,𝑏 grp summarizes the batch-size-dependent similarity terms that enter the PŁ Lyapunov recursion.

Rather than reporting a separate ablation, the batch-size grid search of Appendix E.6 navigates this tradeoff—per-iteration cost 𝒪 (𝑚 + 𝑏 grp ) growing with 𝑏 grp , the 𝛿 2-term minimized at 𝑏 grp = 𝑚 , and the 𝛿 1-term removed only at 𝑏 grp = 𝑛 —empirically, selecting the 𝑏 grp that minimizes oracle complexity on each instance. 68 E Supplementary Experimental Details

Our theory predicts that optimization is highly sensitive to this choice: it is beneficial to group the data so that the within-group similarity is high (small 𝛿 2), while the influence of across-group similarity ( 𝛿 1) depends on the dimensional regime ( 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 versus 𝑛 > 𝑚 ). To empirically validate our theory, we generate synthetic grouped datasets that natively respect the nested finite-sum structure of (62) . We investigate two aspect ratios, (𝑛,𝑚 ) = (50 ,250) and 

(𝑛,𝑚 ) = (250 ,50) , corresponding to the 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛 and 𝑛 > 𝑚 regimes, respectively. For each aspect ratio, we construct four datasets that realize all four quadrants of the qualitative similarity space: 

(𝛿 1 small , 𝛿 2 small ), (𝛿 1 small , 𝛿 2 large ), (𝛿 1 large , 𝛿 2 small ), (𝛿 1 large , 𝛿 2 large ).

The datasets are generated directly in their grouped form, so a sample (𝑖,𝑗 ) belongs to group 𝑖 by construction and no post-hoc clustering step is required. To evaluate methods that operate on a single-level finite sum, the same data are also stored in flattened format. The base task is the binary logistic regression with the nonconvex regularizer 𝑟 (𝑥 ) := ∑︀ 𝑑 ℓ=1 𝑥 2 

> ℓ

𝑓 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) = log (︀ 1 + exp( −𝑦 𝑖,𝑗 𝑎 ⊤ 

> 𝑖,𝑗

Because the same regularizer 𝑟 (𝑥 ) is added to every component, it cancels exactly in the Hessian differences used to define 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2, while continuing to contribute to the global and maximum smoothness constants 𝐿 and 𝐿 max .The feature vectors 𝑎 𝑖,𝑗 are generated using a hierarchical latent-mixture model. We define 𝐾 latent components partitioned evenly into 𝑇 macro-families, such that each family contains 𝑞 = 𝐾/𝑇 

components. We let 

𝑡 (𝑘 ) := 1 + ⌊︂ 𝑘 − 1

𝑞 ⌋︂ , 𝑘 ∈ [𝐾 ],

denote the macro-family index of component 𝑘 . We sample 𝑇 + 𝐾 orthonormal directions 

𝑣 1, . . . ,𝑣 𝑇 ,𝑢 1, . . . ,𝑢 𝐾 ∈ R𝑑 

and establish the cluster centers as: 

𝑐 𝑟 := 𝑟 inter 𝑣 𝑟 , 𝜇 𝑘 := 𝑐 𝑡 (𝑘 ) + 𝑟 intra 𝑢 𝑘 .

The scalar 𝑟 inter dictates the separation between distinct macro-families, while 𝑟 intra controls the variance among latent components within the same macro-family. Each group 𝑖 ∈ [𝑛 ] is assigned a prototype mixture vector 𝛽 𝑖 ∈ R𝐾 . To prevent degenerate zero probabilities when sampling, we smooth this prototype using a small constant 𝜀 dir :̃︀ 

𝛽 𝑖 = (1 − 𝜀 dir )𝛽 𝑖 + 𝜀 dir 

1

𝐾 .

The data points are then sampled hierarchically: 

𝜋 𝑖 ∼ Dirichlet( 𝛼 dir ̃︀ 𝛽 𝑖 ), 𝑧 𝑖,𝑗 ∼ Categorical( 𝜋 𝑖 ), 𝑎 𝑖,𝑗 ∼ 𝒩 (𝜇 𝑧 𝑖,𝑗 ,𝜎 2obs 𝐼 𝑑 ).

All datasets are generated in dimension 𝑑 = 1000 . The (𝑛,𝑚 ) = (50 ,250) configuration uses seed 

42 , and the (𝑛,𝑚 ) = (250 ,50) configuration uses seed 142 .Labels are generated via a Gaussian teacher logistic model. The teacher direction 𝑥 ⋆ is normalized and rescaled by the inverse median absolute raw logit to ensure class probabilities do not degenerate. Labels are sampled according to: 

P(𝑦 𝑖,𝑗 = 1 | 𝑎 𝑖,𝑗 ) = 11 + exp( −𝑎 ⊤

If the empirical positive-class fraction falls outside the [0 .3, 0.7] range, the teacher direction is resampled. To formalize the four structural regimes, let 𝑒 𝑘 denote the 𝑘 -th standard basis vector in R𝐾 , and define the base indices:

Analogously, 𝛿 2 is small when each 𝜋 𝑖 concentrates on one local component (or on nearby components) and large when each 𝜋 𝑖 spreads mass over well-separated components. The Dirichlet concentration parameter 𝛼 dir controls how closely each 𝜋 𝑖 tracks its prototype 𝛽 𝑖 . Since 

E[𝜋 𝑖 ] = ̃︀ 𝛽 𝑖 ,

large values of 𝛼 dir force the realized mixtures 𝜋 𝑖 to concentrate around the prescribed prototypes. The small smoothing parameter 𝜀 dir only prevents degenerate zero entries in the Dirichlet parameters and has negligible effect on the intended geometry. We now interpret each of the four regimes in turn. 72 Small 𝛿 1, Small 𝛿 2. All groups share the same tightly concentrated prototype 

𝛽 𝑖 = 0 .95 𝑒 1 + 0 .05 𝑒 2.

Up to small Dirichlet fluctuations, every group then has the same mixture; hence 𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ) is close to 

𝐻 (𝑥 ) for every 𝑖 , and 𝛿 1 is small. The prototype is itself nearly pure, and the geometry parameters use very small observation noise and within-family radius, 

𝜎 obs ∈ { 0.01 ,0.005 }, 𝑟 intra ∈ { 0.02 ,0.01 }.

Consequently, samples within each group concentrate around almost the same latent component, so the matrices 𝐻 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) stay close to their local average 𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ) and 𝛿 2 is small. The large values of 𝛼 dir 

and the very small 𝜀 dir = 10 −4 further suppress accidental mixture variability. 

Small 𝛿 1, Large 𝛿 2. All groups again share the same prototype, but the prototype is now broad, 

𝛽 𝑖 = 1

𝑇 

> 𝑇

∑︁ 

> 𝑟 =1

𝑒 𝑎 𝑟 .

Because the same mixture is used for every group, the group Hessians 𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ) remain close to each other and to 𝐻 (𝑥 ), so 𝛿 1 stays small. Each group, however, now mixes one active component from every macro-family. The macro-family centers are separated at scale 

𝑟 inter = 25 for 𝑚 ≥ 𝑛, 𝑟 inter = 30 for 𝑛 > 𝑚, 

and the directions defining the macro-centers are orthonormal. For components belonging to different macro-families, the corresponding rank-one matrices are therefore substantially different: at the level of the idealized centers, if 𝑟 ̸ = 𝑟 ′, the matrices 𝑐 𝑟 𝑐 ⊤ 

> 𝑟

and 𝑐 𝑟 ′ 𝑐 ⊤ 

> 𝑟 ′

have operator-norm separation of order 𝑟 2inter . As a result, within a single group, the component Hessians 𝐻 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) fluctuate strongly around 𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ), producing a large 𝛿 2. The concentration values 𝛼 dir ∈ { 1000 ,1500 } keep this broad mixture stable across groups, preventing 𝛿 1 from becoming comparable to 𝛿 2.

Large 𝛿 1, Small 𝛿 2. The group prototype now depends on the assigned macro-family 𝑠 (𝑖 ):

𝛽 𝑖 = 0 .95 𝑒 𝑎 𝑠 (𝑖 ) + 0 .05 𝑒 𝑎 𝑠 (𝑖 )+1 .

Different groups are thus anchored to different macro-families. Since the macro-centers are separated by 𝑟 inter ∈ { 25 ,30 }, the corresponding group-level Hessian averages are far apart, and the terms 

𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ) − 𝐻 (𝑥 ) become large on average; this is the mechanism producing a large 𝛿 1.At the same time, each group is nearly pure within its assigned family, with very small within-family radius and observation noise, 

𝑟 intra ∈ { 0.02 ,0.01 }, 𝜎 obs ∈ { 0.01 ,0.005 }.

Therefore, although different groups are far from one another, samples inside a fixed group concentrate around a single local component. Hence 𝐻 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) stays close to 𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ) within each group and 

𝛿 2 remains small. This regime cleanly isolates between-group heterogeneity from within-group heterogeneity. 

Large 𝛿 1, Large 𝛿 2. The last regime combines the two mechanisms above. We enlarge the latent dictionary to 𝐾 = 64 and the number of macro-families to 𝑇 = 8 , giving more distinct family identities. For each family we define 

𝛾 𝑟 = 12 𝑒 𝑎 𝑟 + 12 𝑒 𝑎 𝑟 +4 ,

so that 𝛾 𝑟 already mixes two distinct components inside macro-family 𝑟 . The group prototypes are 

𝛽 (𝑟 ) = 0 .7𝛾 𝑟 + 0 .3𝛾 𝑟 +1 , 𝛾 𝑇 +1 = 𝛾 1,𝛽 𝑖 = 𝛽 (𝑠 (𝑖 )) .

The dominant term 0.7𝛾 𝑠 (𝑖 ) gives each group a distinct macro-family anchor: since 𝑠 (𝑖 ) varies across groups, the group Hessians 𝐻 𝑖 (𝑥 ) have different dominant macro-family contributions, which drives 𝛿 1 large. The secondary term 0.3𝛾 𝑠 (𝑖 )+1 guarantees that every group also mixes an additional 73 separated macro-family. Together with 𝑟 inter = 30 and 𝑟 intra = 0 .5, this yields substantial variation among the component Hessians inside each group, keeping 𝛿 2 large as well. This regime is deliberately more structured than a symmetric broad mixture. A fully symmetric broad mixture would make every group internally diverse but too similar to every other group, yielding large 𝛿 2 but only moderate 𝛿 1. The cyclic family-anchored construction avoids this by assigning each group a different dominant family while retaining internal diversity through the secondary family and the two-component mixtures 𝛾 𝑟 .

E.3 Estimating 𝐿 , 𝛿 1, 𝛿 2 in theory 

We first record an explicit upper bound on the Lipschitz smoothness constant of the logistic regression objective (1) obtained directly from the component Hessian in (69) . Because the regularizer is nonconvex, the relevant quantity for smoothness is the operator norm of the Hessian, not only a Loewner upper bound. We therefore bound ⃦⃦ ∇2𝑓 (𝑥 )⃦⃦ op term by term. The logistic part of the component Hessian is positive semidefinite, and since 𝛼 𝑖,𝑗 (𝑥 ) ≤ 14 for all 𝑥 , 𝑖 , and 𝑗 , averaging over the 𝑛𝑚 samples and using monotonicity of the operator norm on PSD matrices gives ⃦⃦⃦⃦⃦⃦

For the similarity constants 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2, a closed-form expression of this kind is not available. Instead, we provide an equivalent characterization of the gradient-difference conditions (71) – (72) as operator-norm constraints on stacked Hessian deviations. This characterization both clarifies how the data-generation mechanisms of Section E.2.1 translate into the magnitudes of 𝛿 1 and 𝛿 2, and motivates the empirical estimation protocol of Section E.4.

This perspective guides both the partition design described above and the empirical measurement protocol introduced next.

E.5 Hardware and implementation details 

All experiments were implemented in Python 3.12.8 using PyTorch 2.5.1 (CUDA 11.8 backend, cuDNN 9.1.0). Execution was performed on a workstation featuring dual Intel Xeon Gold 6246 CPUs (24 physical cores, 48 logical threads) alongside an NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3090 (24 GB) and an RTX 2080 Ti (12 GB). 78 Method Stepsize formula

E.6 Hyperparameter tuning E.6.1 Stepsizes 

For all methods in the main synthetic comparison, we used the largest theoretically admissible constant stepsize. Concretely, after fixing the final batch size from the batch-tuning stage (and, for 

D-ZeroSARAH , the final pair (𝑠,𝑏 ) of client subset size and local batch size), we substituted these values into the theoretical stepsize expressions. SILAGE (𝑚 > 𝑛 ) involves no batch-size tuning; for SILAGE (𝑛 > 𝑚 ) we plug in the selected batch size 𝑏 grp ; for flattened ZeroSARAH and SILVER 

we plug in the selected flat minibatch size 𝑏 ; and for D-ZeroSARAH we plug in the selected pair 

(𝑠,𝑏 ). For flattened ZeroSARAH and D-ZeroSARAH , whose finer-grid runs did not reach the target tolerance within the 40 -epoch budget, the selected batch size (or pair (𝑠,𝑏 )) is the one chosen by the fallback rule described below, namely the configuration achieving the smallest final squared gradient norm at the end of the 40 -epoch budget. The resulting formulas are summarized in Table 7, and the corresponding numerical values are listed in Table 8. All numerical values are reported rounded to six significant digits. Table 8: Numerical constant stepsizes used in the synthetic experiments, obtained by evaluating the formulas of Table 7 at the final selected batch size 𝑏 and, where applicable, client subset size 𝑠 . A dash (—) indicates that the corresponding hyperparameter is not used by the method.

E.6.2 Batch-size tuning 

We used method- and setting-specific grids for batch-size tuning. No batch-size tuning was performed for SILAGE (𝑚 > 𝑛 ). For SILAGE (𝑛 > 𝑚 ) in the 𝑛 > 𝑚 setting, we searched over 

{1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 51 , 56 , 61 , 66 , 71 , 76 , 81 , 86 , 91 , 96 ,

101 , 106 , 111 , 116 , 121 , 126 , 131 , 136 , 141 , 146 , 151 , 156 , 161 , 166 , 171 , 176 ,

181 , 186 , 191 , 196 , 201 , 206 , 211 , 216 , 221 , 226 , 231 , 236 , 241 , 246 , 250 }.

For flattened ZeroSARAH , in both the 𝑚 > 𝑛 and 𝑛 > 𝑚 settings, we searched over 

{1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 64 , 96 , 128 , 192 , 256 , 384 , 512 ,

768 , 1024 , 1536 , 2048 , 3072 , 4096 , 6144 , 8192 , 10240 , 12500 }.

For SILVER , we used the combined grid 

{1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 50 , 64 , 96 , 128 , 192 , 256 , 384 , 512 ,

768 , 1024 , 1536 , 2048 , 3072 , 4096 , 6144 , 8192 , 10240 , 12500 },

whose lower part had already been computed and whose upper part was added later. For D-ZeroSARAH ,we used setting-dependent grids: in the 𝑚 > 𝑛 setting, 

𝑏 ∈ { 1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 56 , 66 , 76 , 86 , 96 , 111 , 126 , 151 , 176 , 201 , 226 , 250 },𝑠 ∈ { 1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 50 },

where 𝑏 is the local batch size and 𝑠 is the client subset size; in the 𝑛 > 𝑚 setting, 

𝑏 ∈ { 1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 50 },𝑠 ∈ { 1, 6, 11 , 16 , 21 , 26 , 31 , 36 , 41 , 46 , 56 , 66 , 76 , 86 , 96 , 111 , 126 , 151 , 176 , 201 , 226 , 250 }.

Whenever a tuning grid contained runs that reached the target tolerance within the 40 -epoch budget, we selected the final batch size (or pair (𝑠,𝑏 ) for D-ZeroSARAH ) as the one requiring the smallest number of epochs to converge. For the finer-grid ZeroSARAH and D-ZeroSARAH sweeps, none of the runs reached the target tolerance within 40 epochs; in those cases, we applied a fallback rule and selected the configuration with the smallest final squared gradient norm at the end of the budget. 80 Regime 𝑏 grp
